Dear Sir/ Madam, 
 
Subject: Notice of the Tenth (10th) Annual General Meeting (“AGM”) and Integrated 
Annual Report for the Financial Year 2025-2026 comprising of Business Responsibility 
and Sustainability Report of ESAF Small Finance Bank Limited (“Bank”) – Intimation 
under Regulation 34 and 58 of Securities and Exchange Board of India (Listing 
Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015  
 
In continuation to our intimation dated July 10, 2026, we wish to inform that the Tenth (10th) 
Annual General Meeting (“AGM”) of the Shareholders of ESAF Small Finance Bank Limited 
(the “Bank”), will be held on Friday, August 14, 2026 at 3.00 P.M (IST) through Video 
Conferencing (“VC”) / Other Audio-Visual Means (“OAVM”), in compliance with relevant 
circulars issued by the Ministry of Corporate Affairs (“MCA”) and the Securities and Exchange 
Board of India (“SEBI”), from time to time. 
 
Pursuant to Regulation 34 and 58 of the SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure 
Requirements) Regulations, 2015, please find enclosed herewith, the Annual Report of the 
Bank for the financial year 2025-2026 comprising of Business Responsibility and Sustainability 
Report, along with the Notice of AGM which is being sent through electronic mode to the 
Members of the Bank whose email addresses are registered with the Registrar and Share 
Transfer Agent of the Bank/Depository Participant(s). 
 
Further, in terms of Regulation 44 of Securities and Exchange Board of India (Listing 
Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015 (“SEBI Listing Regulations”) and 
Section 108 of the Companies Act, 2013 read with the Companies (Management & 
Administration) Rules, 2014 as amended, the Bank is providing the facility to its Members 
holding shares as on the cut-off date, August 7, 2026 to exercise their right to vote by electronic 
means (the ‘Remote e-voting’) on any or all of the business specified in the accompanying 
notice. 
 
 
 

===== PAGE 2 =====
 
 
 
 
 
The facility for e-voting will also be made available during the AGM, and those shareholders 
present in the AGM through VC/OAVM facility, who have not cast their vote on the resolutions 
through remote e-voting and are otherwise not barred from doing so, shall be eligible to vote 
through the e-voting system at the AGM. 
 
The Notice of the AGM and Annual Report comprising of Business Responsibility and 
Sustainability Report for the Financial Year 2025-26 are also made available on the website 
of the Bank at https://www.esaf.bank.in/investor-relation/?id=annual-reports.

DISCIPLINED 
STEPS, 
STRONGER 
OUTCOMES.
Transformation begins with 
intent, but it is discipline 
that turns intent into impact 
and over the past year, at 
ESAF Small Finance Bank 
we remained resolute to 
strengthen our organization 
and make it more resilient for 
the future.
Measured strategic decisions 
and consistent focus on 
execution have helped us move 
decisively towards stability 
and improve our business and 
financial performance.
This progress is not the result 
of any single decision, but the 
cumulation of deliberate, well-
placed, and calibrated steps 
percolated throughout the 
entire organisation.
These include a sharper 
focus on improving operating 
efficiency, proactively 
managing credit risks, 
creating a granular retail 
deposit base, prioritizing asset 
quality by diversifying portfolio 
to increase secured loan 
mix, investing in technology, 
expanding our presence, and 
deepening customer engagement.
These changes will enable us to 
deliver superior customer service, 
de-risk loan book, improve 
profitability and margins while 
strengthening balance sheet and 
yielding higher return ratios.
We believe that every measured 
step taken with discipline 
and commitment, allows us 
to navigate challenges with 
confidence and will ultimately 
lead to the path of progress, 
enduring strength, and shared 
prosperity. This will ultimately 
enable us to reinforce our position 
as a stable and trustworthy 
partner for all our customers and 
lay a strong foundation for the 
sustainable long-term growth of 
our organization.

THINKING.  
PURPOSEFUL 
REPORTING. 
Our Integrated Annual Report presents a 
comprehensive view of our value creation 
approach and strategic direction, set against the 
backdrop of an evolving external environment. 
Reporting Frameworks
This Report has been prepared 
with reference to the International 
Integrated Reporting Framework, as 
well as the Business Responsibility 
and Sustainability Reporting 
(BRSR) framework mandated 
by the Securities and Exchange 
Board of India (SEBI) under the 
Listing Obligations and Disclosure 
Requirements (LODR) Regulations, 
2015.
In adhering to the nine guiding 
principles of the BRSR framework, 
the Bank reinforces its commitment 
to embedding Environmental, 
Social, and Governance (ESG) 
considerations into its strategy and 
operations.
Reporting Scope & Boundary
1st April 2025 to 31st March 2026
Responsibility
The Board is of the view that 
this Integrated Annual Report 
comprehensively addresses all 
material matters relevant to the Bank 
and provides meaningful insights 
into our approach. The Board also 
acknowledges the integrity of the 
Report’s content, which has been 
developed under the guidance 
and oversight of the Bank’s senior 
management.

Integrated Annual Report

Dear Stakeholders,
Every institution is defined not 
only by the heights it achieves, but 
by how it responds to moments of 
challenge and transformation. At 
ESAF Small Finance Bank, we have 
embraced this past year as an 
opportunity to reset, recalibrate, 
and rebuild with greater strength 
and clarity of purpose. What 
we are witnessing today is the 
emergence of a more resilient, 
disciplined, and future-ready 
organisation taking shape.
This has been a year of meaningful 
transition, marked by resilience, 
disciplined execution, and an 
unwavering focus on long-term 
sustainability. I am pleased 
to note that we have made 
significant progress across 
our key strategic parameters. 
This progress reflects both the 
strength of our approach and the 
cooperation of our customers in 
shaping and building your pursuits.
India Steady in 
a Volatile Global 
Economy
The global economy entered the 
year 2026 under conditions of 
heightened uncertainty, with 
escalating geopolitical tensions. 
Despite the VUCA effect, India 
has held its ground, reporting 
GDP growth of 6.5% on the 
strength of domestic demand 
and a few structural reforms 
initiated by the Government 
of India. Currently, currency 
depreciation and higher crude oil 
prices are weighing on external 
balances, but these pressures 
are largely cyclical and global in 
origin rather than reflective of any 
At ESAF Small Finance Bank, we have 
embraced this past year as an opportunity 
to reset, recalibrate, and rebuild with 
greater strength and clarity of purpose.
domestic weakness. Continued 
policy stability and focus on 
macroeconomic fundamentals will 
be key to sustaining momentum.
Amidst the evolving global 
environment, including the 
conflict in West Asia, the Bank 
has demonstrated remarkable 
resilience and successfully 
steered the institution in the right 
direction.
Customer milestone
The progress we have made 
this year rests on a foundation 
built over years: the trust of our 
customers and the commitment 
of our employees, added to 
deepening presence across 
various states of our nation.
In this context, I am pleased to note 
that we have crossed a significant 
milestone of 1 crore customers 
within nine years of operations. This 
achievement reflects not only the 
scale we have built, but also the 
strength of our outreach and the 
confidence that customers place 
in us as a trusted financial partner. 
We have focused on strengthening 
our fundamentals, improving 
asset quality, and building a more 
resilient, diversified asset model, 
resulting in a long-term structural 
transformation.

===== PAGE 8 =====
Focus on Secured 
Lending
Central to this transformation 
is our shift towards increasing 
secured lending under the MARG 
framework coined as an acronym 
for the loans given to MSME, 
Agriculture, Retail, and Gold 
sectors. These segments have 
shown strong growth in 2025-26 
as compared to the previous year 
and they have an added advantage 
of stable asset quality with low 
NPAs, contributing to a more 
diversified and de-risked portfolio. 
A growing share of secured assets 
is improving recoverability and 
reducing earnings volatility. 
Secured assets now form 61% of 
total gross advances. The most 
tangible outcome has been a 
significant improvement in asset 
quality and reduction in provisions.
With credit costs better controlled 
and portfolio stability improving, 
we returned to quarterly 
profitability in Q3 of 2025-26 with 
PAT of `7 crore and improved it 
further in Q4 2025-26 with PAT of 
`24 crore. We believe that we will 
see this trend of positive financial 
performance continuing in the 
coming quarters as well. 
Strengthening 
Fundamentals
We remain firmly committed to 
our core principles of financial 
inclusion and serving the 
underserved segments. Our 
microfinance business will continue 
to grow in a responsible and 
calibrated manner, supported by 
stronger collection mechanisms, 
and enhanced monitoring.
Our growth strategy has remained 
measured and quality-focused 
throughout the year with the aim 
of balanced growth across both 
sides of the balance sheet. Thus, in 
2025-26, both the gross advances 
and deposits have grown steadily 
and sequentially every quarter 
from Q1 to Q4 of 2025-26. While 
on the asset side, we focused on 
de-risking and diversifying our 
portfolio by increasing our secured 
lending, on the liabilities side, 
we continued to strengthen our 
granular retail deposit franchise. 
As of 31st March 2026, the Bank’s 
capital adequacy ratio stood 
strong at 22.2% and the Liquidity 
Coverage Ratio stood at 143.4%, 
both well above the regulatory 
limits, indicating a comfortable 
liquidity position and ensuring 
both stability and financial agility 
for future growth. 
Our products and services are built 
around a deep understanding of evolving 
customer needs, ensuring accessibility, 
relevance, and real impact.
8
Integrated Annual Report 
2025-26
Customer Centricity
Our products and services are 
built around a deep understanding 
of evolving customer needs, 
ensuring accessibility, relevance, 
and real impact. Our strength 
lies in our deep presence across 
rural and semi-urban markets 
and our ability to build trust-
based personalised relationships 
with customers by providing 
exceptional customer service. 
We firmly believe that this 
relationship-led approach to 
growing together is powerful. 
In parallel, we remain committed 
to supporting our customers’ 
financial well-being and therefore 
continue to invest in regular 
financial literacy programmes. The 
initiative is to increase customer 
awareness and encourage them 
to adopt responsible, secure 
banking habits. The broader aim 
of such initiatives is to encourage 
customers to maintain a strong 
credit profile by educating 
them on timely repayments 
and safeguarding their future 
borrowing capacity by ensuring 
disciplined credit behaviour 
thereby supporting long-term 
portfolio health.
We are intensely focused on 
maintaining asset quality in the 
standard category, which also 
helps customers maintain a 
positive credit score.
People
We appreciate the strong 
cooperation and commitment 
displayed by all our employees 
during this challenging year. We 
have taken various initiatives on 
capacity building, digital enablement 
and continuous learning and 
development to empower our teams.
We look forward to this continued 
collaboration going ahead as well 
that will keep us on our growth 
momentum and at the same time 
ensure we continue to exceed our 
customer expectations.  
Technology
Technology is central to our next 
phase of growth, and we have 
undertaken a comprehensive 
transformation through ESAF 
2.0 - StratoNext Programme, to 
be implemented before end of this 
calendar year 2026. The platform 
will enhance customer experience, 
improve turnaround times, better 
data governance, improve risk 
management and strengthen 
operational efficiency. Integrating 
digital capabilities with our 
physical network will allow us to 
scale with greater precision while 
deepening customer engagement.
Moving Forward with 
Confidence
As we look ahead, our focus on 
improving asset quality, building 
a more balanced portfolio, 
and strengthening operating 
performance gives us confidence 
in delivering sustainable 
financial performance. Also, 
our governance and regulatory 
framework is aligned with global 
best practices, supported by 
continuous monitoring.
We remain committed to 
expanding secured lending, 
deepening our deposit base, 
enhancing digital capabilities, and 
maintaining strong risk discipline, 
while staying true to our core 
mission of financial inclusion.  With 
a stronger foundation and a clear 
strategic direction, we are well 
positioned to create long-term 
value as a trusted, responsible 
institution.
I am deeply grateful to every 
stakeholder who has been part 
of this journey: our shareholders, 
customers, employees, Board 
of Directors, and business and 
technology partners. Your 
support, commitment, and belief 
in what we are building have been 
instrumental in every step of our 
progress. We look ahead with 
confidence and optimism and 
create sustained value for all our 
stakeholders in the years ahead.
I would also like to acknowledge 
the guidance and encouragement 
of our regulatory authorities, 
particularly the Reserve Bank 
of India, SEBI, the Insurance 
Regulatory and Development 
Authority of India, and the 
Pension Fund Regulatory and 
Development Authority, along 
with the Government of India and 
various state governments. Their 
support continues to play a vital 
role in strengthening the financial 
ecosystem and enabling our 
relevance and expansion.
Thank you for your trust and 
encouragement.
Warm regards,
Shri. Karthikeyan 
Manickam, 
Chairman

Managing Director’s 
Message
Small Finance Banks in 
India serve as crucial 
catalysts for deepening 
financial inclusion 
across unbanked 
rural and semi-urban 
populations. 
Dear Stakeholders,
It is easy to measure progress in 
numbers; what matters more is 
what those numbers represent. 
At ESAF Small Finance Bank, we 
remain conscious that economic 
growth, by itself, is not enough. 
Our focus is on ensuring that 
growth translates into meaningful 
impact for the communities we 
serve by enabling society to 
prosper as a whole.
With this perspective in mind, we 
look at the broader economic 
environment that continues 
to evolve. India continues to 
demonstrate resilience, with the 
United Nations projecting GDP 
growth of 6.4% in the year 2026 and 
6.6% in the year 2027, reinforcing 
its position as a key driver in the 
Asia-Pacific region. Growth in year 
2025 stood at 7.4%, supported by 
strong consumption, particularly 
in rural markets, aided by GST rate 
reductions and export momentum 
ahead of anticipated tariff changes 
in the United States. However, the 
ongoing geopolitical instability 
in the Middle East has clouded 
the immediate global economic 
outlook. The resultant volatility in 
crude oil prices is posing moderate 
headwinds to input costs, and its 
inflationary effects will remain a 
key monitorable factor to predict 
India’s economic growth in the 
coming year.
On the policy front, the Reserve 
Bank of India in the Monetary 
Policy Meeting (MPC) held in April 
2026, maintained the repo rate at 
5.25%, following cumulative rate 
cuts of 125 basis points during 
2025–26. Driven by moderating 
inflation and steady growth, this 
easing cycle has improved liquidity 
conditions and provided tangible 
relief to borrowers. But in lieu of 
the recent global developments, 
policymakers are expected to 
prioritize stabilizing the rupee and 
managing bond yields, rather than 
immediate rate hikes.
Small Finance Banks (SFBs) in 
India serve as crucial catalysts 
for deepening financial inclusion 
across unbanked rural and 
semi-urban populations. The 
middle- and lower-income groups 
constitute a significant portion of 
India’s economy. SFBs can serve 
as a crucial bridge in generating 
employment opportunities within 
these segments, with special focus 
on empowering women borrowers 
by providing them with earning 
opportunities. SFBs also promote 
micro-entrepreneurship and by 
expanding credit to underserved 
enterprises, play a huge role 
in supporting MSMEs, which 
contribute to 30% of India’s GDP. 
Agriculture as a sector remains 
a primary contributor to India’s 
economy. SFBs can leverage 
government initiatives such as the 
Pradhan Mantri Kisan Maandhan 
Yojana and help extend financial 
security and benefits to farmers, 
further strengthening the sector.
Our focus is on ensuring that growth 
translates into meaningful impact for the 
communities we serve enabling society to 
prosper as a whole.
1 crore
Customers
9 years
of Operations
We therefore believe that SFBs will 
play a pivotal role in advancing 
the vision of Viksit Bharat 2047 by 
enhancing rural per capita income 
and reducing rural disparities by 
providing specialised, affordable 
credit and modern technology-
driven digital banking services.
Through all of this, the role and 
strategic direction of ESAF 
Small Finance Bank remains 
clear. By expanding access to 
finance, enabling livelihoods, 
and strengthening grassroots 
enterprises, we seek to contribute 
to a more balanced and inclusive 
growth story for India.
The Year in a Nutshell
2025–26 can be defined as a 
transition year for us, marked by 
a conscious recalibration of our 
business model amid the sector-
wide microfinance stress that was 
prevalent over the last fiscal year. 
As a result, for us the fiscal year 
2025-26 was effectively a year 
of two halves. While the first half 
was impacted by the challenging 
operating environment, the second 
half saw a clear improvement in 
performance, driven by portfolio 
rebalancing towards more secured 
assets, better asset quality, 
improved operating efficiency, and 
financial performance.
ESAF Small Finance Bank

===== PAGE 10 =====
Our priorities were aligned 
towards resilience and laying the 
groundwork for a sustainable 
growth path. We took corrective 
actions to effectively change the 
portfolio mix and improve asset 
quality, used data and analytics 
for disciplined underwriting and 
risk monitoring, and invested in 
expanding the franchise for last 
mile customer engagement and 
service. The objective was clear - 
transforming and building a more 
stable and resilient institution.
Central to this transformation 
has been the execution of our 
MARG strategy. MARG in Sanskrit 
signifies direction, and this 
framework reflects our clear 
strategic path towards secured 
lending led by MSME, Agriculture, 
Retail, and gold loans. This 
framework is the cornerstone 
of our portfolio de-risking 
approach. An added advantage 
of this portfolio transition is that 
all the constituent segments 
under the MARG framework 
exhibit significantly lower NPA 
levels. Hence, during the year, 
we consciously focussed on and 
accelerated growth across our 
secured segments, resulting in 
gross advances from the MARG 
portfolio growing by 47% as 
compared to the previous year. 
As of 31st March 2026, secured 
advances form 61% of total gross 
advances and we remain on track 
to achieve our stated target of 
70% secured assets by 31st March 
2027.
Microfinance, on the other 
hand, remains a core part of 
ESAF’s DNA. During the year, 
the microfinance operating 
environment was continuously 
evolving, with tighter regulatory 
expectations, SRO guardrails, 
greater focus on borrower 
leverage limits, and strengthened 
collection discipline across the 
industry. These developments 
had a bearing on credit flows 
and disbursement momentum 
but also laid the foundation 
for a more responsible and 
sustainable ecosystem. The sector 
is now showing some signs of 
stabilisation as overall collection 
efficiencies have improved, and 
operating conditions are gradually 
normalising. This encourages us 
to believe that in the coming year, 
we may see some growth in this 
sector.
We adopted a focused and 
calibrated strategy for our 
microfinance portfolio during the 
year as we continued to place 
strong emphasis on regulatory 
alignment, stricter underwriting 
norms and customer protection. 
As a result, the microfinance 
portfolio in 2025-26 remained 
stable in absolute terms, but its 
share reduced to 39% of total 
gross advances as compared to 
47% last year.
Beyond credit, we have deepened 
our engagement with customers 
through credit-plus services, 
financial literacy (more than 2,600 
programmes), and community-
based initiatives. These initiatives 
help us in educating customers 
about responsible credit, 
ultimately strengthening financial 
behaviour and supporting long-
term borrower sustainability.
On the liability side, we made 
steady progress in deepening 
our retail deposit franchise, 
with a particular focus on CASA 
growth across rural and semi-
urban markets. Retail deposits 
remained strong at 92% of total 
deposits. Our strategy remains 
centred on deposit granularity 
and a stable funding base, limiting 
reliance on bulk deposits. We 
are leveraging our franchise 
strength in Kerala and South 
India, alongside longstanding 
NRI relationships to support 
consistent deposit mobilisation. 
During the year, we crossed a 
milestone of 1 crore customers 
During the year, we consciously focussed on 
and accelerated growth across our secured 
segments, resulting in gross advances from 
the MARG portfolio growing by 47% as 
compared to the previous year.

Integrated Annual Report 
2025-26
with a strong representation of 
women borrowers. We see this as 
a structural strength of our model, 
contributing to both portfolio 
resilience and long-term franchise 
value. 
This strength stems from our 
strong distribution network we 
have built over a period of last 9 
years, spanning 24 states and 2 
union territories, with a majority 
presence across rural and 
semi-urban areas. It is anchored 
by our 804 banking outlets and 
720 ATMs, ensuring consistent 
access to banking services across 
geographies.
The Business Correspondent 
model plays a central role 
in last-mile delivery, credit 
outreach, deposit mobilisation 
and customer engagement. We 
have 32 institutional business 
correspondent partners and 
700 plus business facilitators 
operating 1,000 plus customer 
service centres. Going forward, we 
are seeing a great opportunity to 
grow our business by leveraging 
the network we built and the 
products we have developed 
across all our customer segments. 
Performance in 
Numbers
Microfinance segment stress 
drove elevated GNPA and NNPA 
levels during the earlier part of 
the year.  We responded with 
timely, decisive action to stabilise 
credit quality. These included 
shift in portfolio mix, disciplined 
underwriting, selective sale of 
stressed assets to ARCs, higher 
provisioning, and disciplined 
collection mechanisms. These 
efforts are yielding results, with 
visible improvement in asset 
quality. GNPA is now at 5.4% 
compared to 6.9% last year and 
NNPA is at 1.8% compared to 3.0% 
last year. The slippage ratio on a 
gross basis reduced from 10.5% in 
We responded with timely, decisive action to 
stabilise credit quality. These included shift 
in portfolio mix, disciplined underwriting, 
selective sale of stressed assets to ARCs, 
higher provisioning, and disciplined collection 
mechanisms. 
2024-25 to 6.5% for 2025-26.
Gross advances rose from `18,779 
crore in 2024–25 to `22,426 
crore in 2025–26, reflecting a 
year-on-year growth of 19%. 
Disbursements for the year more 
than doubled and reached `42,530 
crore, compared to `20,984 crore 
in the previous year.  Secured 
lending constituted 83% of total 
disbursements.
Total deposits stood at `25,850 
crore, registering a year-on-year 
growth of 11% over `23,276 crore. 
Retail deposits recorded a healthy 
growth of 9%, increasing from 
`21,640 crore to `23,674 crore.

Technology-led

is a comprehensive overhaul of our 
technology landscape, building a

The StratoNext initiative, 
expected to go live by the end

strengthen our core capabilities 
across risk management,

experience. Our focus on data

key priority and complement our 
digital transformation journey. 
The aim is to make a digital first 
experience accessible to all, 
while reaching out to the next 
generation of customers.
We have initiated steps to

Charting the Future
2025-26 was a year of 
consolidation for us. As we enter 
the next fiscal year 2026-27, we 
will continue to build on our last 
year’s performance in a more 
disciplined and structured way 
to ensure sustainable growth. 
Our priorities are clear - asset 
quality normalisation and 
improving return ratios. A shift 
in portfolio mix towards secured 
assets, sustained operational 
efficiency, moderating slippages 
and improved collection 
efficiencies, supported by tighter 
credit discipline, will drive these 
outcomes. 
We are working towards 
expanding our geographical 
presence both on the liability and 
asset side and driving incremental 
growth beyond the home state, 
enabling a more balanced and 
resilient presence.
We will continue to strive towards 
our long-term strategic direction 
and vision of evolving into a 
well-diversified retail-led social 
bank. This vision is built on 
customer centricity, financial 
inclusion, and the creation of 
enduring stakeholder value. Every 
strategy, product, and partnership 
is designed to advance this 
purpose, with an eventual 
aspirational pathway to universal 
banking. We will strive to achieve 
this goal while remaining faithful 
to ESAF’s social purpose and 
staying rooted in our mission of 
inclusive growth. 
We are deeply grateful to our

trust and continued support. 
Their confidence strengthens

not just an institution, but a 
movement that advances financial 
inclusion and empowerment 
across the nation.

About the Bank
The ESAF journey began in 1992 
with the establishment of a 
Non-Governmental Organisation 
dedicated to fostering sustainable 
development among marginalised 
communities. Inspired by 
the Grameen Bank model of 
Bangladesh, Dr. K. Paul Thomas, 
MD & CEO and Individual 
Promoter of the Bank, introduced 
microenterprise development 
services in 1995. This initiative 
paved the way for the formation 
of ESAF Microfinance and 
Investments Private Limited in 
2008, creating a more structured 
platform for expanding these 
activities. Over the years, 
these efforts evolved into a 
comprehensive financial services 
framework, ultimately leading to 
the establishment of ESAF Small 
Finance Bank.
Enabling Financial 
Growth. Step by 
Step. 
We prioritise lending to productive 
sectors that contribute meaningfully 
to the creation of goods and services, 
in line with the Reserve Bank of 
India’s (RBI) priority sector lending 
requirement that directs 60% of our 
portfolio towards priority sectors.
20
Integrated Annual Report 
2025-26
Vision  
To be India’s leading social bank that 
offers equal opportunities for the whole 
society through universal access and 
financial deepening, thus promoting 
financial inclusion, livelihood, and 
economic development as a whole.
Mission 
To provide responsive banking services 
to the underserved and unserved 
households in India, facilitated by 
customer-centric products, high-quality 
service, and innovative technology.
Values
Servant Leadership
Customer Centric
Accountability

Triple Bottom Line Approach
Guided by the principles of People, Planet, and 
Prosperity, our Triple Bottom Line approach 
reflects our deep commitment to strengthening 
livelihoods, enabling sustainable economic growth, 
and serving low- and middle-income segments 
across India. Anchored in our guiding motto, 
‘fighting the partiality of prosperity,’ we strive to 
build a more equitable and inclusive society where 
opportunities are accessible to all. 
Prosperity
Ensuring the prosperity of all stakeholders 
in a sustainable manner

communities at large and enhancing overall

Retail Assets
Retail Assets caters to the 
diverse financing needs of 
retail customers by offering 
housing, mobility, personal, and 
social infrastructure financing 
products, enabling asset creation, 
fulfilling consumption needs, and 
enhancing quality of life. 
The segment is built around 
mortgage-backed lending, mobility 
finance, and general retail credit 
solutions, supporting customer 
aspirations across housing, 
mobility, and personal financial 
needs. Within the Bank’s broader 
strategic transition towards a 
more diversified and increasingly 
secured portfolio, retail lending 
continues to play an important 
role in improving product breadth, 
customer relevance, and portfolio 
resilience.
36
Integrated Annual Report 
2025-26
Markets Served 
The product suite for this segment may be grouped into three principal categories: 
Mortgage Loans
• 
Loan Against Property for lawful personal needs 
such as education, marriage, healthcare, debt 
consolidation, and other family requirements. 
• 
Housing-linked offerings such as Dream Home 
Loan and Micro Housing Loan, covering purchase, 
construction, extension, renovation, and balance 
transfer. 
• 
Lease Rental Discounting (LRD Loan) against 
monthly rent receivables from self-owned 
premises.
Social Infrastructure Loans
• 
Financing solutions designed to support the 
development and enhancement of social 
infrastructure, with a focus on educational 
institutions, student financing, and skill 
development initiatives that contribute to 
community advancement and human capital 
development.
•

Creating Impact
Crunching Their Way to Success
Neethu, & Rekha 
Kozhikode District, Kerala
In the quiet village of Edachery 
near Vadakara, three women—
Nimala, Neethu, and Rekha—
decided to take destiny into their 
own hands. Driven by a shared 
dream of financial independence 
and a better future for their 
families, they set out on an 
entrepreneurial journey. Their 
trusted companion along the 
way was ESAF Small Finance 
Bank. Through ESAF’s Income 
Generation Loan (IGL), they 
each secured `50,000— enough 
to kickstart their venture. With 
unwavering determination, they 
founded the Surya Small Scale 
Value Addition Unit, specialising 
in traditional Kerala snacks like 
jaggery sweets, banana chips, 
vellayappam, and pazham pori. 
Operating from a humble shed 
near Nimala’s house, they did 
everything themselves—from 
cooking and packaging to 
delivery. Their flavours spoke for 
themselves. As their snacks gained 
popularity in nearby markets, 
demand soared. With rising 
orders, they expanded operations, 
hiring two more staff members 
and creating new employment 
opportunities. Securing an FSSAI 
licence and business registration 
ensured their unit’s long-term 
growth and sustainability. 
From homemakers to business 
owners, their journey became 
one of courage, teamwork, and 
empowerment. The income they 
generated improved their families’ 
lives and inspired other women in 
the community to follow suit. With 
ESAF’s support, they did not just 
make snacks—they made history 
in their own small way. Nimala, 
Neethu, and Rekha are among the 
many people who started dreaming 
big with ESAF.
Watt’s in a Transformation! 
Jitesh Jayvantrao 
Pendam 
Yavatmal District, Maharashtra
In the rural stretches of Kalamb 
taluka, Jitesh Jayvantrao Pendam, 
who holds an ITI certificate, found 
himself caught in the classic 
dilemma of being educated yet 
unemployed. With limited job 
opportunities in his village, Jitesh 
worked as a local electrician, 
earning just `2,000 to `3,000 a 
month—hardly enough to support 
his family of four. Their five acres 
of non-irrigated farmland offered 
little respite, plagued as it was by 
unpredictable weather and poor 
yields. Hope arrived in the form 
of Urjabandhu, a solar technician 
training programme organised by 
Prachodhan Development Services, 
a CSR implementing agency of 
ESAF Small Finance Bank. Seeing a 
chance to redirect his future, Jitesh 
joined the programme. The hands-
on training covered solar and 
electrical basics, installation and 
fabrication techniques, and even 
entrepreneurship development 
through Entrepreneurship 
Development Programme (EDP) 
workshops. The learning did 
not stop at the classroom. At a 
placement meeting organised by 
Prachodhan, Jitesh was one among 
the 12 Urjabandhu trainees selected 
for internships and jobs out of the 
18 applicants. He landed a role 
at Nirmal Agro Solutions with a 
monthly salary of `23,000—almost 
eight times what he used to earn. 
Today, Jitesh is more than just 
employed—he is empowered. His 
career transformation has uplifted 
his family’s standard of living and 
aligned his technical skills with 
real-world needs. His story proves 
that with the right training and 
opportunity, rural youth can shine 
just as brightly as the solar panels 
they install. 
44
Integrated Annual Report 
2025-26
Sewing Seams of Strength
Anita 
Chikkaballapur District, Karnataka
In the small village of 
Nimmakalakunte, Chikkaballapur, 
Anita lived with her husband, 
Nandeesh, and their two 
children, facing a constant battle 
with financial instability. Her 
husband’s daily wages barely 
covered household needs, and 
Anita longed to contribute more 
meaningfully to the family’s 
income. The turning point came 
when a friend introduced her to 
free tailoring classes—a spark 
of hope in an otherwise difficult 
life. With determination, Anita 
saved what little she could and, 
along with small loans, purchased 
a basic tailoring machine. She 
began earning `200 to `300 a day, 
modest yet impactful. But her 
dreams did not stop there. Having 
previously faced an unfavourable 
experience with moneylenders, 
she was initially hesitant to scale 
her business. That changed 
when she discovered ESAF Small 
Finance Bank. Encouraged by 
the guidance of ESAF staff, Anita 
availed a loan of `40,000, which 
she used to purchase modern 
electric sewing machines. Her 
income grew to `10,000 to 
`15,000 per month, enabling her 
to provide better education for 
her children. As her confidence 
and trust in the business grew, she 
availed a second loan of `70,000, 
allowing her to expand her setup 
and employ two neighbours. 
Today, Anita’s business earns 
her ` 20,000 a month, with daily 
transactions between ` 2,000 and 
`3,000. Her tailoring shop has 
become both a livelihood and a 
symbol of hope. She now dreams 
of expanding further, employing 5 
to 10 people from her community, 
and continuing to stitch together a 
future of possibilities. 
From Soil to Success
Sumathi  
Sivaganga District, Tamil Nadu
In the quiet village of Themmavayal 
in Sivaganga district, Sumathi’s 
story begins with hardship but 
grows into one of determination, 
transformation, and prosperity. 
Alongside her husband, China 
Karupaiya, Sumathi worked as 
a daily wage labourer, despite 
owning farmland that was left 
underutilised due to lack of capital. 
With no means to develop the land, 
they struggled to meet even their 
basic needs. Everything changed 
when Sumathi learned about the 
agriculture loans offered by ESAF 
Small Finance Bank. After meeting 
with ESAF staff, she followed 
their advice and formed a Joint 
Liability Group (JLG) of 10 women 
in Veppankulam. Together, they 
availed a Farmer Interest Group 
(FIG) loan of `50,000. With the 
newly secured capital, Sumathi 
purchased farming seeds and 
began cultivating her own land 
with the help of her husband. What 
started as a small beginning soon 
grew into a successful farming 
venture. With each harvest, 
their income increased, bringing 
financial stability to the family 
and newfound respect within the 
community. Today, Sumathi earns 
over `20,000 per month through 
farming. She has even begun 
arranging transportation for her 
produce, further strengthening 
her business. From struggling to 
survive to becoming a community 
leader, Sumathi’s journey stands as 
a powerful reminder that with the 
right support, small beginnings can 
lead to big success.

Multi-Nodal Delivery Channels
Our multi-nodal delivery model ensures efficient and inclusive access to banking services across diverse 
customer segments. Our branch network remains the primary channel for customer engagement and service 
delivery, supported by a micro banking channel comprising Business Correspondents and agents that help 
extend our reach, particularly in underserved areas. This physical presence is complemented by advanced 
digital platforms, including internet and mobile banking, enhancing service efficiency and reflecting our focus 
on continuous technological advancement. Supported by a growing ATM network our delivery channels 
collectively strengthen accessibility, convenience, and the overall customer experience across geographies.

Banking Outlets in Rural and Semi-Urban Areas
Strong Rural and Semi-Urban Banking Franchises
In regions where access to formal banking remains limited, particularly across rural and semi-urban markets, 
we have established a deep and purposeful presence. Our focus is on extending credit to underserved 
communities by addressing their distinct financial requirements and supporting local economic activity. We

Continued Focus on Secured Asset Portfolio

mitigated asset portfolio. This is reflected in the steady increase in secured disbursements, underscoring our 
emphasis on prudent portfolio construction and sustainable growth. By prioritising secured lending, we have 
reduced risk exposure, improved capital efficiency, and strengthened the long-term resilience of our business 
model.

MARG Strategy
Building a 
Secure and 
Resilient 
Portfolio 
The year 2025–26 marked a critical 
inflection point in our strategic journey, 
driven by the need to strengthen 
balance sheet resilience, improve 
asset quality, and diversify earnings 
in line with our stated strategy, which 
is now increasingly embedded in our 
business activities. Building on the 
previous year’s focus, we formalised 
our emphasis on MSME, Agri, Retail, and 
Gold loans under our MARG strategy. 
‘Marg,’ meaning ‘the way’ in Sanskrit, 
represents a structured framework 
guiding us towards more diversified and 
secured loan book.
This approach addresses the evolving needs of 
unbanked and underbanked customer segments. 
It also aligns with our long-term objective of 
achieving sustainable and profitable growth, 
without losing sight of our mission of inclusive 
banking across rural and semi-urban markets
Core Pillars of the 
MARG Strategy
Under this pillar, we continue to 
support MSMEs as a key driver 
of India’s economic growth by 
focussing on granular ticket 
sizes, localised underwriting, 
and closer monitoring. This is 
done by leveraging our strong 
branch presence across Tier 2 
and 3 cities and rural markets. We 
have emphasised secured MSME 
lending, including loans backed by 
property, business assets, cash 
flows, and structured collateral, 
primarily catering to micro 
and small enterprises in trade, 
services, and manufacturing. 
This approach enables us to 
achieve better risk-adjusted 
returns compared to unsecured 
lending, while also contributing 
meaningfully to our priority sector 
lending (PSL) objectives.
1
MSME Lending
We have leveraged our deep rural 
franchise and long-standing 
community relationships to expand 
responsibly in agri lending. Our 
portfolio spans farm, allied, and 
agri-linked non-farm activities, 
including dairy, fisheries, and 
associated value chains. Recognising 
the multi-fold impact of collectives, 
we have extended credit support to 
FPOs and promoted the adoption of 
climate-smart agriculture practices 
through various initiatives. Our 
loans in this segment are largely 
asset- or cash flow-backed, which 
helps reduce volatility and enhance 
portfolio stability.
2
Agri and Allied Activities
56
Integrated Annual Report 
2025-26
Under retail lending, 
we have focussed on 
secured products such as 
affordable housing, loan 
against property (LAP), and 
auto loans. Our strategy 
emphasises sustainable loan 
growth while maintaining 
stringent asset quality 
standards. This segment 
supports predictable cash 
flows and helps smooth 
earnings cyclicality across 
economic cycles.
3
Retail Secured Lending
Gold loans have emerged as 
a significant growth driver 
under our MARG framework 
in 2025–26, supported by 
strong demand in rural 
and semi-urban markets. 
The portfolio has recorded 
robust year-on-year and 
quarter-on-quarter growth, 
reinforcing gold loans as a 
highly liquid, short-tenor, and 
secured lending product. We 
have also enhanced product 
design, turnaround time 
and risk controls to support 
scalable yet prudent growth 
in this segment.
4
Gold Loans
Impact on Financial 
Performance and 
Asset Quality
The MARG strategy has 
strengthened our financial 
performance and asset 
quality through 2025–26. The 
MARG portfolio continues to 
demonstrate strong asset 
quality, with gross NPA remaining 
below 1%. We witnessed a 
meaningful improvement in asset 
quality metrics, with a significant 
reduction in both Gross and 
Net NPAs. Credit costs also 
stabilised, supported by a higher 
proportion of secured assets and 
improved recovery performance. 
Our Net Interest Margin also 
improved, reflecting a more 
efficient and resilient earnings 
profile. Notably, we returned to 
profitability in 2025–26, with the 
turnaround clearly driven by 
our disciplined execution of the 
MARG strategy and continued 
focus on portfolio de-risking.
Portfolio 
Rebalancing and 
Risk De-Risking
As part of our MARG-led 
transformation, we have 
undertaken a deliberate 
reduction in our unsecured 
microfinance exposure to 
lower concentration risk. 
Microfinance now represents 
a smaller and more calibrated 
share of our total advances, 
with a sharper focus on 
individual borrowers and 
improved collection efficiency. 
Secured assets under the MARG 
framework accounted for over 
57% of our total advances by 
2025–26, marking a significant 
increase from the previous year 
and highlighting clear progress 
in our portfolio transformation. 
We have also set a medium-term 
target of achieving around 70% 
secured advances, reflecting 
our strong commitment to 
structural risk reduction.
Enablers Supporting 
the MARG Strategy
Forward-Looking 
Perspective
We believe that the MARG strategy 
positions ESAF Small Finance Bank 
for sustainable, profitable, and 
resilient growth, while carefully 
balancing our growth aspirations 
with strong asset quality discipline. 
Going forward, our focus remains 
on deepening MARG penetration 
across geographies, enhancing 
cross-sell opportunities, and 
maintaining robust risk controls. 
This framework will remain 
fundamental to our business 
strategy, enabling us to align long-
term shareholder value creation 
with our commitment to financial 
inclusion and responsible banking.
Risk Management  
We have strengthened our credit 
appraisal processes, enhanced 
collateral valuation practices, and 
implemented robust early warning 
systems to effectively manage 
secured portfolios.
Distribution 
We continue to leverage our 
widespread branch network and 
relationship officers to drive 
localised, relationship-based lending.
Technology  
We have aligned our operations 
with ongoing digital and core 
banking transformation initiatives, 
enabling scalability and facilitating 
real-time monitoring.
Governance  
We maintain strong oversight 
through senior management and 
the Board to ensure disciplined 
adherence to our targeted portfolio 
mix, defined risk thresholds, and 
overall quality of growth.

Financial 
Capital 
During 2025–26, the Bank maintained a strong capital adequacy position and improved asset quality, while profitability 
remained under pressure due to margin compression and elevated credit costs. Our focus remained on calibrated 
growth, strengthening the secured portfolio mix, improving liability quality and deploying capital with discipline across 
priority and inclusion-led segments. We continued to advance our transition towards a more balanced asset mix 
through the MARG framework and a clear roadmap to increase the share of secured assets in the portfolio.

2025–26
Our financial position in 2025–26 was characterised by growth in total assets to ` 30,867.98 crore, an 
improvement in Net NPA to 1.8%, and a robust CRAR of 22.2%. At the same time, margin compression and credit 
costs continued to weigh on profitability, reflected in a Net Interest Margin of 6.4%, Return on Assets of (0.6)% and 
Return on Equity of (9.1)%. 
On the liabilities side, the Bank continued to strengthen the quality of its funding franchise. Retail deposits 
remained strong at 92% of total deposits and increased to `23,674 crore as on 31st March 2026, from `21,654 
crore as on 31st March 2025. Total borrowings rose to `2,753 crore from `1,406 crore, while the average cost of 
borrowings moderated to 8.0% from 8.6% in the previous year.

Technology, 
Resilience and 
Climate Readiness
Technology is a core element of 
our manufactured capital and 
enables secure, scalable, and 
efficient banking operations. 
We continue to strengthen our 
operating backbone through 
investments in core banking 
systems, digital service platforms, 
resilient network infrastructure, 
and data management 
capabilities.
Our technology and infrastructure 
priorities include integrated 
service delivery across branch, 
assisted, mobile, and digital 
channels; resilient systems 
supported by disaster recovery 
arrangements; paperless and 
automated workflows; and 
cybersecurity and access 
controls aligned with regulatory 
expectations.
Operational resilience is embedded 
across critical infrastructure 
through backup power, alternate 
connectivity, secure asset 
arrangements, and geographically 
separated disaster recovery 
facilities. These capabilities help 
maintain continuity of essential 
services during disruptions. 
We regularly conduct business 
continuity and disaster recovery 
drills and use the learnings to 
strengthen infrastructure and 
technology readiness.
Sustainable and 
Resource-Efficient 
Infrastructure
Environmental considerations 
are reflected in energy-efficient 
lighting, optimised space 
utilisation, workflow digitisation, 
and responsible procurement 
practices across branches and 
offices.
Digitisation and process 
automation are also reducing 
paper use and physical storage 
requirements, supporting both 
operational efficiency and 
resource optimisation. We 
continue to evaluate opportunities 
to adopt greener infrastructure 
practices, including renewable 
energy solutions and lower-
impact operating models where 
feasible. We seek to improve the 
environmental efficiency of our 
infrastructure while maintaining 
service effectiveness and 
resilience.
Manufactured Capital 
and Value Creation
Physical infrastructure supports 
our ability to create long-term 
value by expanding access, 
improving continuity, and 
enabling more efficient service 
delivery. Physical infrastructure 
remains essential in underserved 
markets, while assisted and 
digital channels extend reach with 
lower incremental infrastructure 
intensity.
Our approach is guided by four 
priorities: wider access, stronger 
resilience, greater efficiency, 
and responsible infrastructure 
development. This includes 
gender-responsive service 
environments, climate-aware 
infrastructure planning, and digital 
enablement that broadens reach 
without proportionate physical 
expansion.
Looking ahead we will continue 
to strengthen our manufactured 
capital base through a disciplined 
combination of physical presence,

Under ESAF 2.0, we launched a comprehensive digital transformation 
initiative called StratoNext to modernise our core technology landscape and 
strengthen enterprise-wide digital capabilities. 
The initiative transitions us from legacy systems to an API-driven 
architecture that enhances speed, scalability, resilience, and security. 
StratoNext focuses on infrastructure modernisation, enterprise application 
migration, and cybersecurity-first principles. By doing so, it creates a 
regulatory-compliant, high-availability technology environment capable of 
supporting mission-critical banking operations. 
ESAF 2.0 is also helping us strengthen our institutional and digital capacity 
to deliver high-performance, customer-centric financial services while 
accelerating automation and innovation across digital channels.

• 
Deploying advanced analytics for improved decision-making and credit 
assessment 
• 
Integrating systems to improve turnaround time and service efficiency 
•

•

Sustainability 
Roadmap 2023–27
Our Sustainability Roadmap 
2023–2027 guides the Bank’s 
approach to integrating green 
finance, operational efficiency, and 
resource stewardship into long-
term value creation. The roadmap 
is anchored in three priorities: 
expanding green finance, optimizing 
renewable energy, electric mobility, 
and climate-resilient livelihoods. 
The roadmap gives thrust to 
operational efficiency through 
energy optimisation and reduced 
paper use; and strengthening 
resource stewardship through 
water conservation and waste 
reduction practices.
Green Finance and 
Customer Transition
The Bank’s natural capital 
strategy is expressed most 
directly through its green and 
inclusion-linked lending products. 
These include the Suryajyothi 
solar loan for rooftop solar 
adoption, electric vehicle financing 
across customer segments, the 
Nirmal and Jeevadhara loans 
supporting sanitation and water 
infrastructure, and Farmer 
Interest Group loans that support 
small and marginal farmers in 
climate-sensitive livelihoods. 
Together, these offerings connect 
environmental priorities with 
customer-level resilience and 
responsible growth.
Operational 
Environmental 
Stewardship
Environmental responsibility is 
embedded across key elements 
of the Bank’s operations. ESAF 
Bhavan, the Bank’s Corporate 
and Registered Office is supported 
by a 105-kW solar power plant 
generating approximately 
1,80,000 kWh annually. Energy-
efficient infrastructure, including 
LED lighting, inverter-based 
air-conditioning, and BEE star-
rated equipment, supports 
lower operating intensity. Digital 
processes, e-documentation, and 
workflow automation continue to 
reduce paper use across customer 
interactions and internal processes. 
Water-efficiency practices, 
responsible procurement, and 
sustainable event protocols further 
support operational stewardship. 
The Bank has also institutionalised 
a Green Protocol supported 
by annual Green Audits across 
branches and departments, 
covering energy conservation, 
water efficiency, waste reduction, 
and environmental housekeeping. 
A dedicated Water Protocol 
reinforces responsible water 
use and links internal practices 
with customer-facing solutions 
that support water access and 
conservation.

Our CSR strategy is guided by 
community needs, regulatory 
expectations, and our commitment 
to creating meaningful and long-term 
social impact, particularly among 
underserved and economically 
vulnerable populations. Drawing on 
our extensive grassroots presence, 
we design and implement community

and economic well-being while 
strengthening local resilience and 
inclusion.
Through Sangam Initiatives, we 
strengthened member engagement, 
enabled deeper community

cohesion. Community initiatives

Customer Trust and 
Service Excellence
Customers remain central to the 
Bank’s engagement philosophy. 
The Bank engages with customers 
through branches, relationship 
managers, calls, emails, SMS, 
WhatsApp, digital platforms, 
surveys, and customer interaction 
forums. These channels support 
better understanding of customer 
expectations across product 
relevance, accessibility, service 
quality, transaction security, and 
grievance redressal. 
The Bank continued to strengthen 
service quality across touchpoints 
during the year. ISO 9001:2015 
certification has been achieved 
for customer service quality 
initiatives, regulatory reporting, 
grievance redressal, and call 
centre monitoring. The Bank was 
also recognised as Small Finance

Financial Inclusion 
and Community 
Development
Financial inclusion remains 
central to the Bank’s social capital 
approach, delivering accessible 
and responsible financial services 
to underserved and unbanked 
populations, particularly women, 
rural households, and small 
entrepreneurs. Joint Liability 
Group and Self-Help Group 
models support last-mile credit 
access and livelihood resilience, 
while the Bank’s branch and 
Business Correspondent network 
extends access to savings, 
remittance, insurance, direct 
benefit transfer, and basic banking 
services.
Community engagement is 
guided by needs on the ground, 
regulatory expectations, and a 
commitment to long-term social 
impact. Through community-
focused initiatives, the Bank 
supports four broad areas: 
financial literacy and inclusion, 
livelihood enhancement, education

well-being. Sangam initiatives 
also supported deeper member 
engagement and community 
participation during the year.

and Awareness
The Bank’s financial literacy efforts 
complement access to banking 
and credit by improving awareness 
of savings, budgeting, responsible 
borrowing, digital banking, fraud 
prevention, insurance, and financial 
planning. During the year, the Bank 
conducted 2,649 financial literacy 
programmes, reached 56,497 
customers through these efforts, 
and conducted 42 customer 
awareness programmes. Balajyothi 
reached 14,821 school students, 
while Vayojyoti reached 5,897 
senior citizens with safe banking 
and digital awareness messages.

Robust 
Governance. 
Resilient 
Banking. 
Strong governance, ethical conduct, and 
integrity are the foundation of everything 
we do. These principles guide stakeholder 
relationships and enable us to move steadily 
towards our vision of becoming India’s most 
respected Social Bank. Our governance 
approach is rooted in fairness, transparency, 
and accountability. We strictly adhere to all 
applicable laws, Listing Regulations, and RBI 
directions.
Governance 
Within this framework, risk 
management operates as a 
continuous, organisation-wide 
discipline rather than a standalone 
control function. Oversight begins 
at the top, with the Board and 
Senior Management actively 
guiding our risk direction. Clearly 
articulated policies, structured 
procedures, and defined risk 
limits establish boundaries for 
responsible growth, while well-
developed systems enable timely 
identification, assessment, and 
monitoring of risks across all 
business activities.
Robust Management Information 
Systems and a comprehensive 
internal control environment 
strengthen this structure, ensuring 
that emerging risks are detected 
early and addressed effectively. 
Independent Risk Management, 
Compliance, and Internal Audit 
functions provide an additional 
layer of assurance and help shape 
an annual risk strategy aligned 
with the Bank’s risk appetite, 
capital planning, and performance 
goals.
Major risk areas are governed 
through specialised committees. 
Credit exposures, liquidity and 
interest rate positions, operational 
resilience, business continuity, 
and information security are 
overseen by dedicated forums 
with defined mandates. This 
ensures that expertise is applied 
where it matters most, while also 
embedding risk perspectives into 
key management decisions.
84
Integrated Annual Report 
2025-26

Governance

within our core risk framework 
and informs the way we manage

and evolving regulatory

priorities, measurable objectives, 
and time-bound targets. This 
helps us ensure that ESG 
considerations are integral to 
decision-making rather than 
treated as standalone initiatives.
This integrated approach 
enhances resilience and risk 
mitigation, thereby positioning 
the Bank as a proactive 
force for positive social and

Management Discussion & Analysis
INDIAN ECONOMY OVERVIEW
India’s economic trajectory for 2025-26 reflected a phase 
of confident expansion, with GDP growth rate reported at 
7.6%, marking a clear step up from the prior year’s 6.5%. 
The expansion was driven by a strengthening consumption 
cycle and accelerating manufacturing activity. Despite a 
fragmented global trade environment shaped by evolving 
geopolitical alignments, India’s domestic growth engines 
have gained depth and resilience. Tax rationalisation 
and the maturation of digital public infrastructure have 
deepened this resilience, cementing India’s standing.
Growth was broad-based across sectors. Agriculture 
remained on a steady footing, supported by the Digital 
Agriculture Mission and targeted programmes for self-
reliance in pulses. Industrial output gained traction, with 
manufacturing emerging as a standout contributor. The 
services sector retained its position as the primary growth 
engine, underpinned by record levels of digitally delivered 
services and an ambitious push to expand India’s share 
in global services trade. Merchandise exports navigated 
external headwinds with resilience, while strong capital 
inflows helped contain the current account deficit. Foreign 
exchange reserves stand at around $690 billion, according 
to Reserve Bank of India (RBI) data as of May 2026. While 
the headline figure is among the world’s largest reserve 
holders, there is growing sensitivity to global commodity 
prices and valuation changes on account of the developing 
geopolitical concerns.
During the year, GST 2.0 was launched leading to a major 
overhaul of India’s tax system and simplifying the structure 
into two main slabs—5% and 18%—with a 40% rate for 
luxury/sin goods. The key objective of the reform was to 
reduce compliance cost on tax filing, make MSMEs more 
competitive and boost consumer demand by lowering tax 
on daily essential goods.
The Reserve Bank of India’s (RBI) Monetary Policy 
Committee held the repo rate steady at 5.25% in its April 
2026 review, following a cumulative reduction of 100 basis 
points through 2025–26. The pause reflects a measured 
response to persistent global uncertainties, particularly 
risks stemming from geopolitical conflicts and resultant 
effect on energy markets. Domestic inflation moderated 
sharply to 2.0%, preserving room for policy support without 
compromising macroeconomic stability. The neutral 
stance balances the need to sustain credit growth against 
the imperative of insulating the economy from external 
volatility.
The financial system has strengthened materially, with gross 
non-performing assets (GNPA) declining to a multi-decade 
low of 2.1% by late 2025. This reflects improved credit 
discipline and the structural impact of insolvency reforms. 
However, there are also risks. Climate variability affecting 
agricultural output and global commodity price fluctuations 
are key concerns. For 2026–27, growth is projected to 
moderate to 6.8%–7.2%, supported by a recovery in rural 
demand and sustained focus on macroeconomic stability 
and investor confidence. The Viksit Bharat@2047 vision 
anchors this trajectory in inclusive development, with 
structural reforms and fiscal prudence.
INDIAN BANKING SECTOR OVERVIEW
India’s Banking, Financial Services, and Insurance (BFSI) 
landscape is undergoing a structural transformation, 
shaped by technology adoption, evolving regulatory 
frameworks, and strong domestic credit momentum. 
As a key enabler of the Viksit Bharat@2047 vision, the 
sector is expanding financial inclusion while strengthening 
institutional resilience and efficiency.
Key Trends
• 
Consolidation 
and 
Capital 
Strengthening: 
The sector is in a renewed phase of consolidation. 
Strategic mergers are creating large, well-capitalised 
institutions better equipped to support complex 
infrastructure and long-term investment cycles. 
Public sector banks have demonstrated notable 
profitability, strengthening their balance sheets and 
capital adequacy. This positions them with greater 
capacity to absorb economic shocks.
• 
Digitalisation with Embedded Security: Digital 
banking has evolved into a security-centric ecosystem. 
Regulatory emphasis has led to widespread adoption 
of 
advanced 
authentication 
mechanisms 
and 
biometric verification across platforms. The rapid 
expansion of digital payment systems reflects both 
scale and trust. Non-Banking Financial Companies 
(NBFCs) and small finance banks (SFBs) are leveraging 
AI to develop innovative credit assessment models 
that extend formal finance to underserved rural 
segments.
• 
Focussed Credit Expansion: Credit growth remains 
broad-based, with a clear policy focus on strengthening 
the MSME segment. Revised classification norms and 
targeted financial products are improving access to 
institutional credit for smaller enterprises. Enhanced 
guarantee mechanisms and tailored lending solutions 
are helping address long-standing financing gaps, 
while improved asset quality reflects stronger credit 
discipline and more effective risk management 
practices across the sector.

SMALL FINANCE BANKING SECTOR OVERVIEW
SFBs have evolved from basic inclusion mandates to 
delivering technology-enabled credit solutions across 
customer segments, establishing themselves as integral 
pillars of India’s financial system. In 2025–26, their 
evolution is marked by a decisive shift from microfinance-
centric operations to comprehensive, digitally integrated 
banking platforms designed for emerging and underserved 
customer segments. SFBs are enabling wider economic 
participation through tailored offerings across various 
segments.
Their footprint has expanded with the rise of phygital 
banking models, combining on-ground presence with 
digital accessibility. With a dominant share of their 
branches located in unbanked rural and semi-urban 
centres, SFBs have become key enablers of government-
backed livelihood and enterprise initiatives. AI-led credit 
assessment frameworks have improved risk evaluation 
and operational efficiency, enabling deeper penetration of 
credit services. By channelling rural savings into regulated 
financial systems, SFBs are gradually displacing informal 
lending ecosystems and fostering long-term financial 
security. This transformation has been largely supported 
by the adoption of technology as internet penetration has 
widened access, allowing customers in even the remotest 
areas to access financial services like mobile banking and 
digital payments anytime, anywhere.
On the regulatory front, RBI has provided a supportive 
operating environment. One such initiative during the 
year was the decision to lower Priority Sector Lending 
requirements for small finance banks from 75% to 60%. 
It is a welcome development, offering greater portfolio 
flexibility, improved capital allocation, and enhanced risk 
diversification to the industry.
The continued expansion of SFBs is redefining financial 
inclusion, transforming previously excluded populations 
into active contributors to the formal economy. SFBs are 
supporting capital formation at the grassroots and carrying 
economic progress to the most remote regions through a 
stable, technology-enabled banking ecosystem. 
MICROFINANCE SECTOR OVERVIEW
Microfinance remains a cornerstone of India’s financial 
inclusion framework in 2025–26, extending credit, savings, 
and insurance to segments traditionally excluded from 
formal banking. Anchored in the Joint Liability Group 
(JLG) model, microfinance institutions (MFIs) support over 
100 million households, with women constituting nearly 
98% of the borrower base. Regulatory refinements by 
the RBI in 2022 and 2025 have reinforced this evolution. 
Household-level income assessments are now central to 
promoting responsible lending and preventing borrower 
over-indebtedness.
The sector is, however, undergoing a prudent recalibration. 
Following a phase of aggressive expansion, 2025–26 has 
seen a shift toward a risk-first approach, with the aggregate 
microfinance portfolio stabilising at approximately ` 3.30 
lakhs crore. Elevated credit costs and a temporary increase 
in Portfolio at Risk (PAR 180+), reaching around 15%, 
have prompted institutions to prioritise portfolio quality 
over rapid scale. Lending strategies are becoming more 
selective, with a focus on existing customer relationships 
and optimised loan sizes to balance growth with 
sustainability.
Alongside this recalibration, the operating environment 
has been shaped by emerging disruptions. The rise 
of unregulated digital lenders and localised ‘Karja 
Mukti’ narratives have, weakened repayment discipline 
within borrower groups, posing challenges to the JLG 
framework. In response, the RBI and state authorities 
have strengthened supervisory oversight to address 
coercive practices and predatory lending. These measures 
have, at times, tempered short-term credit expansion. To 
enhance transparency, the sector is increasingly leveraging 
digital public infrastructure (DPI) and account aggregator 
frameworks. This is enabling a more comprehensive 
assessment of borrower indebtedness across formal and 
informal channels.
On the regulatory front also, MFIN Guardrails 2.0, effective 
1st April, 2025, restricted microfinance borrowers to a 
maximum of 3 lenders to curb over-leveraging. These 
voluntary industry standards, adopted by Small Finance 
Banks (SFBs) and NBFC-MFIs, aim for long-term portfolio 
quality but temporarily increased rejection rates, reduced 
collection speeds, and elevated delinquencies.
Early indicators toward the close of the fiscal year, however, 
point to a gradual recovery. Improved collection efficiencies 
and a revival in targeted lending segments, particularly 
among SFBs and NBFC-MFIs, are driving growth in select 
portfolios. This rebound is a reminder of the sector’s 
inherent resilience and its enduring role in advancing 
grassroots economic mobility.
ESAF SMALL FINANCE BANK: OVERVIEW
ESAF Small Finance Bank (‘ESAF Small Finance Bank’, 
‘The Bank’, ‘Our Bank’, or ‘We’) has built its journey on 
a development-first philosophy. The Bank has steadily 
evolved into a regulated banking institution focussed on 
financial inclusion. The origins trace back to 1992, when Dr. 
Kadambelil Paul Thomas, along with a group of like-minded 
individuals, established ESAF Foundation to promote 
micro-enterprises, drive socio-economic progress, and 
improve community health. This vision gained operational 

===== PAGE 49 =====
90
Integrated Annual Report 
2025-26
Management Discussion & Analysis (Contd.)
momentum in 1995 with the launch of microfinance 
initiatives aimed at extending credit access to underserved 
and marginalised communities.
In 2006, Dr. Thomas and his associates acquired the 
Corporate Promoter of ESAF Small Finance Bank, initiating 
a structural shift to scale these efforts. The Foundation 
subsequently transferred its microloan portfolio to the 
Corporate Promoter under a business transfer agreement 
executed on March 31, 2008, enabling greater institutional 
focus and regulatory alignment.
The next phase of evolution came in 2014, when 
the Corporate Promoter obtained NBFC-MFI status, 
strengthening its position within the formal financial system. 
This set the stage for a pivotal transition in 2017, when the 
lending and financing business was transferred to ESAF 
Small Finance Bank under a business transfer agreement 
signed on 22nd February 2017, and made effective on 10th 
March 2017. This marked the formal transition into an SFB.
The Bank remains structurally aligned with the needs of 
the underserved segments and our focus going forward is 
on deepening financial access through disciplined growth, 
responsible banking practices, and technology-driven 
solutions that scale with the communities we serve.
Commitment to the Triple Bottom Line: People, 
Planet, and Prosperity
Our approach to growth is grounded in a Triple Bottom Line 
philosophy that integrates social impact, environmental 
responsibility, and economic progress. This commitment is 
reflected across three core pillars:
• 
People: Expanding access to inclusive banking 
services, particularly for underserved and marginalised 
communities. 
• 
Planet: Promoting sustainable practices to minimise 
environmental 
impact 
and 
support 
long-term 
ecological balance. 
• 
Prosperity: Driving economic empowerment and 
financial inclusion through responsible financial 
solutions and strengthening financial literacy. 
In line with this approach, the Bank has established 
a 
comprehensive 
ESG 
(Environmental, 
Social, 
and 
Governance) framework. It focuses on safeguarding the 
environment, advancing financial inclusion and gender 
equality through targeted offerings, and reinforcing 
governance through accountability, transparency, and 
adherence to recognised ESG standards.
Our Presence
Our network spans 24 states and 2 union territories 
comprising of 804 Banking Outlets, 720 ATMs and 1,047 
Customer Service Centres managed by 32 Institutional 
Business Correspondents, 2,923 Banking Agents, 737 
Business Facilitators, and 10,994 Employees. Our Business 
Correspondents play a key role in sourcing and servicing all 
types of loans with a special focus on microloan customers.
As of 31st March 2026, the Bank crossed a significant 
milestone of serving more than 100 lakh customers. 
We have always maintained a clear strategic focus on 
serving customers in rural and semi-urban markets. Our 
distribution strategy reinforces this focus, with 69% of 
banking outlets located in these geographies, enabling 
deeper penetration and more effective service delivery.

Business Performance
Total Gross advances have grown strongly during the 
year by 19%, with growth supported by majority of the 
product segments. We have made meaningful progress 
in reshaping our portfolio mix in line with our de-risking 
strategy, enabling a greater shift toward secured and 
better-rated assets.
As part of our de-risking strategy, we have defined a clear 
strategic path toward secured lending called ‘‘MARG.’’ 
consisting of MSME, Agriculture, Retail and Gold loans. 
This transition is enhancing portfolio resilience, reducing 
volatility, and supporting sustainable long-term growth. 
An added advantage is that all the constituent segments 
under the MARG framework exhibit significantly lower NPA 
levels, thus helping us improve our asset quality as well.
Together, the MARG portfolio accounts for 57% of our total 
advances and secured assets now constitute 61% of our 
gross advances, compared to 53% as of 31st March 2025. 
For the microfinance portfolio, during the year, we adopted 
a risk-aware approach with disciplined underwriting. As 
a result, the microfinance book has been rationalised 
from ` 8,857 crore in 2024-25 to ` 8,746 crore in 2025-
26. Due to various initiatives undertaken by the bank 
such as strengthening field engagement through Sangam 
process enhancements, targeted sourcing of higher-
quality customers, structured graduation to individual 
lending, and focused resolution of delinquent accounts, 
the microfinance portfolio is on a more stable footing now.
Going ahead, we remain firmly on track to achieve our 
stated target of 70% secured portfolio as a long-term 
plan. Also, the microfinance sector is currently witnessing 
improving operating environment and we will continue to 
grow it responsibly with a calibrated approach.
Yield on Advances for the year 2025-26 moderated to 
15.7% as compared to 18.3% in the previous year largely 
due to the shift in portfolio mix, the pass through of rate 
cuts, higher slippages and non-recognition of income 
on high yielding micro loans. With deposit costs also 
moderating in line with the industry and focus on growing 
the secured asset book, yields on advances are expected 
to remain range bound.
Deposits
Our Bank offers a comprehensive range of liability 
products, including current accounts, savings accounts, 
term deposits, and recurring deposits. The cost associated 
with these liabilities is influenced by external factors like 
market competition, interest rate movements, and liquidity 
conditions in the Indian credit market. Internally, elements 
such as our credit ratings, available credit lines, and ability 
to mobilise low-cost or non-interest-bearing deposits, 
particularly through current accounts, play a key role in 
determining our overall cost of funds.
Our primary source of funding is a cost-efficient deposit 
base, largely driven by retail depositors across India. This 
is supported by the strategic expansion of our branch 
network and the development of tailored financial products. 
We cater to a wide spectrum of depositor segments and to 
serve these segments, we utilise a diversified distribution 
network comprising our banking outlets, Business 
Correspondents, Customer Service Centres and alternative 
delivery channels.  This approach helps us sustain a stable 
and cost-efficient funding structure through deposits.

Our interest income was influenced by the balance of interest-earning advances and their corresponding yields, higher 
slippages in microloans and the change in mix of secured and unsecured loans during the year. Other factors include the 
balance of investments that generate interest income and average interest-earning balances held with the RBI and other 
interbank funds with their respective yields. On the other hand, our interest expenditure is influenced by the average 
balance of total deposits and borrowings with their associated costs. 
On account of the above factors, net interest margin stood at 6.4% for 2025-26 as compared to 8.1% for 2024-25, but 
some improvements were seen from the second half of the fiscal supported by lower cost of funds and reduction in fresh 
slippages. This combined with an improving asset quality and better operational efficiency has helped improve operating 
profitability.

Our operational performance is closely linked to our ability to manage credit quality effectively, which is assessed in part 
through the monitoring of NPAs. In line with RBI guidelines, loans are classified as NPAs based on the period of non-
payment, requiring appropriate provisioning, including for standard assets. NPAs are further categorised into sub-standard, 
doubtful, and loss assets.
To mitigate these risks, we have implemented robust credit appraisal frameworks and disciplined loan disbursement 
processes, supported by continuous monitoring mechanisms. We also utilise advanced data analytics and automated credit 
scoring tools to improve the accuracy and efficiency of credit evaluation. Certain loan segments, including microloans and 
selected retail loans, are unsecured, which elevates credit risk in the absence of collateral. In cases of default, recovery 
options are primarily limited to legal measures.
Net Profit for the Year
As a result of our corrective actions and strategic shift to secured loans and also on account of  provision requirement on 
higher slippages in Micro loan , we were able to return to profitability in the second half of the year and reduce the total 
losses for the year to ` 166 crore compared to ` 521 crore in 2024–25.

Our reserves and surplus are subject to fluctuations driven by changes in share premium from the issuance or repurchase 
of equity shares, and by movements in the profit and loss account reflecting the net profit or loss for the fiscal year. As a 
result of these factors, the Bank’s reserves and surplus decreased to ` 1,264 crore.
We have leveraged the longstanding strength of the ESAF brand, built over more than three decades. This brand recognition 
has significantly contributed to the rapid growth of our deposit portfolio since the commencement of our operations. We 
made steady progress in deepening our retail deposit franchise, specially across rural and semi-urban markets. These 
deposits are beneficial as they typically carry lower interest rates compared to bulk deposits. Retail deposits remained 
strong at 92% of total deposits and have increased to ` 23,674 crore as on 31st March 2026 from ` 21,640 crore as on 31st 
March 2025.

We meet our liquidity and capital requirements primarily through shareholder capital, customer deposits, and institutional 
borrowings. Over time, we have diversified sources of our borrowings to include funds from the RBI, institutional agencies, 
other banks, subordinated debt, and perpetual debt instruments. As on 31st March 2026, the Bank’s borrowings increased 
to ` 2,753 crore compared to ` 1,406 crore as of 31st March 2025 and the average cost of borrowings stood at 8.0% 
compared to 8.6% as of 31st March 2025.
The Bank’s other Liabilities and Provisions mainly consist of payables, inter-office adjustments (Net), interest accrued and 
provisions including Standard Assets, expenses, etc. The Bank’s other Liabilities and Provisions assets stood at ` 479 crore 
from ` 547 crore as of 31st March 2025.
Financial Instruments and Off-Balance Sheet Arrangements
The Bank transfers advances through interbank participation with and without risk. In accordance with the RBI guidelines, in 
the case of participation with risk, the aggregate amount of the participation issued by the Bank is reduced from advances 
and where the Bank is participating; the aggregate amount of participation is classified under advances. In the case of 
participation without risk, the aggregate amount of participation issued by the Bank is classified under borrowings and 
where the Bank is participating, the aggregate amount of participation is shown as due from banks under advances. During 
the year Bank transferred certain gold loan book  through IBPC on risk sharing basis amounting to `650 Crore and netted 
off with Gross advances in the books of accounts.

OUR BUSINESS SEGMENTS
We operate across four primary business segments: Treasury, Wholesale Banking, Retail Banking, and Other Banking 
Operations.
• 
Treasury: Revenue is primarily generated from interest income on our investment portfolio, gains or losses from 
investment activities, and income from foreign exchange transactions. Major expenses comprise interest paid on 
borrowings and deposits. The segment is also responsible for managing and allocating customer deposits.
• 
Wholesale Banking: We provide credit facilities to corporate clients in compliance with RBI regulations. Revenue is 
derived from interest on corporate loans and fees from banking services, with key expenses comprising interest on 
borrowed funds, deposits, and associated operational costs.
• 
Retail Banking: We serve non-corporate customers through loans and deposit accounts, in line with RBI guidelines. 
Revenue is generated through interest income on loans and fees from banking services, with major expenses comprising 
interest on borrowings, deposits, and related expenditures.
• 
Other Banking Operations: This segment covers income from para-banking activities such as debit card services and 
distribution of third-party products, along with associated costs.
